## 1930s

**1930 — Jan Fryc, "Enterprises and capital in the Polish oil industry", 1930**  
Entry 30. GRIFFEL BENJAMIN IN STANISŁAWÓW. An industrialist owning oil mines at Lipinki and Klimkówka. Entry 58. THE FIRST STANISŁAWÓW FACTORY OF PETROLEUM, MACHINE OIL AND BENZINE OF EISIG CHAIM GRIFFEL. Seated at Stanisławów; a sole firm founded in 1913. Its object is, besides oil mines at Bitków, Pasieczna and Dźwiniacz and an earth-wax mine at Dźwiniacz, the refinery at Mykietyńce near Stanisławów. The refining capacity of the refinery, rebuilt after its destruction in the war, is 18,000 tonnes of crude a year. The sole owner is the industrialist Eisig Chaim Griffel of Stanisławów. The assets of the undertaking also include a holding in the Korneuburger Mineralölraffinerie A.G. at Korneuburg near Vienna (50 per cent of shares). Entry 86. "RAFINERJA SKAWIŃSKA", WORKS FOR THE ROCK-OIL INDUSTRY, GRIFFEL AND CO. Seated at Skawina near Kraków; a general trading partnership founded in 1924 to take over and run the newly built refinery at Skawina. The capacity of this refinery, intended for light grades of crude from western Little Poland, is about 24,000 tonnes a year. The principal owner is EISIG CHAIM GRIFFEL of Stanisławów (80 per cent); the remaining shares belong to JAKÓB GRIFFEL, HIS SON. Entry 87. REFINERY "WIERBIĄŻ" AD KOŁOMYJA, factory of rock-oil products. Seated at Stanisławów; founded in 1921 as a general trading partnership to run the refinery at Wierbiąż. Capacity about 6,000 tonnes of crude a year. Partner and principal owner: EISIG CHAIM GRIFFEL (50 per cent); the rest belongs to Dr Karol Halpern and I. Geller.
*oil industry · the whole empire listed · Jacob a part-owner*

**13 February 1930 — Chwila, year 12, no. 3911 (13 February 1930)**  
PLENARY MEETING OF THE SYNDICATE OF TIMBER INTERESTS IN LWÓW. With very numerous participation of timber producers, industrialists and merchants, the plenary assembly of the Syndicate of Timber Interests was held the day before yesterday in the great hall of the Chamber of Industry and Commerce in Lwów. Opening the sitting, the chairman Dr Cesla regretted that timber affairs and timber policy had passed from the neutral ground of the Ministry of Industry and Trade to the interested Ministry of Agriculture. The policy pursued by the state forests is ruinous both for the national economy and for the Treasury. While all industries have been heavily burdened by taxes, the timber industry has borne, beyond that general burden, a special and heavy sacrifice in the forest levy. Railway tariffs, whose proper regulation could have brought relief, have become a burden. Nor has anything been done for the timber industry in the matter of credit since the Polish state came into being. […] After adoption of the 1930 budget presented by the director of the bureau DR RAPAPORT, the election of the presidium and of members of the section took place. By acclamation Dr Paweł Cesla was elected chairman, and as vice-chairmen engineer Arnold Kolischer, councillor Adolf Kiesler, director Dr Klemens Sokal and councillor Szymon Ulam. To the section were elected councillor Ozjasz Lutwak, Dr Marceli Paneth, Leon Seelig, Dr Józef Thom, director Joachim Thorm, director Januar Treier, director Zygmunt Wolf; and to the audit commission Artur Falter, ZYGMUNT GRIFFEL, director Józef Presser and Fryderyk Spatz.
*timber · trade body*

**March 1930 — Pracownik Drzewny: organ of the trade union of clerks of the timber industry of the Republic of Poland, year 6, no. 3 (March 1930)**  
THEY ARE CUTTING WORKERS' WAGES BY 10 PER CENT. The dance was started, as usual, by the firm "Glaesinger" (Dolina-Krechowice) with its notorious director Pohr at its head. The noble firm uses a tried system of corruption: cutting the wages of all the workers by 10 per cent, it introduces at the same time for a small handful the so-called "laufende Schichten", under which a worker is paid not only for effective working days but also for Sundays and holidays, that is for 30 days a month — so that he does not suffer from the cut but actually gains by it. It costs the firm little, since it concerns only a dozen or so people, and it gains, because it corrupts individuals and breaks workers' solidarity. After weak resistance the workers accepted the wage cut. The firm "Glaesinger" was followed by "GRIFFEL" AT BOLECHÓW, and after them the undertakings belonging to "The Anglo-European" concern: "Dolina" (Dolina-Broszniów), "Industrial Works" (Delatyn), "K.T.L." and "Silwinia" (Wygoda), and finally the firm "Groedel" at Skole (as far as we are informed, only out of class solidarity).
*labour · wage cut*

**23 July 1930 — Gazeta Handlowa, Wednesday 23 July 1930, No. 167**  
COMPOSITION PROCEEDINGS OF THE FIRM GRIFFEL. Great sensation in Little Poland has been caused in recent days by the wavering of the well-known oil firm E. CH. GRIFFEL, which, faced with payment difficulties, is applying for composition proceedings at 50 per cent. The Griffel firm is one of the more considerable among the small Polish refineries; it has refineries in Poland at Stanisławów and Skawina with a joint production capacity of about 400 tank-cars a month, and besides that a refinery at Korneuburg in Austria and also in Romania. We are informed that the Griffel firm suffered above all serious losses on business in Romania, so that its liabilities amount to approximately 11 million, while its assets are only 5 to 6 million złoty.
*bankruptcy · the crash*

**24 July 1930 — Chwila, year 12, no. 4070 (25 July 1930)**  
BANKRUPTCY OF A STANISŁAWÓW MILLIONAIRE. GREAT DEPRESSION AMONG THE CREDITORS. (By telephone from Chwila's correspondent.) Stanisławów, 24 July. Following the declared insolvency of the industrialists EISIG CHAIM AND HIS BROTHER, owners of refineries at Skawina, Stanisławów, Nadwórna and Klosterneuburg, they have applied to the District Court in Kraków for the opening of composition proceedings over their estate. The District Court in Kraków, under number 195/30, has appointed as composition commissioner judge Jaworski and as administrator of the estate the Kraków advocate Dr Woźniakowski. The first hearing in the matter has been fixed at the District Court in Kraków for 28 August, creditors to file their claims by 15 August at the latest. THE AFFAIR HAS CAUSED DEPRESSION IN STANISŁAWÓW, BECAUSE A NUMBER OF PEOPLE, ESPECIALLY FROM THE POORER CLASSES, HAD COMMITTED THEIR PROPERTY TO THE GRIFFELS' BUSINESS. THERE WERE EVEN CASES WHERE SMALLER MERCHANTS DEPOSITED THEIR DAUGHTERS' DOWRIES WITH THE GRIFFELS, HAVING MORE CONFIDENCE IN THE FIRM THAN IN THE BANKS. Yesterday evening a mass meeting of creditors was held in Stanisławów; they will be received today by the composition commissioner, who is coming to Stanisławów specially. According to the declarations the firm's assets amount to 6,400,000 while its liabilities exceed 12 million.
*bankruptcy · the crash · the dowries*

**25 July 1930 — Ilustrowany Kuryer Codzienny, year 21, no. 197 (25 July 1930)**  
ON THE MARGIN OF THE BANKRUPTCY OF A TIMBER FIRM IN STANISŁAWÓW. The news of the bankruptcy of a great timber firm in Stanisławów, which we gave in yesterday's I.K.C., should be understood to mean that it is not a timber firm at all, but the undertaking of one of the most considerable oil industrialists in Kraków, EISIG CHAIM GRIFFEL, owner of a series of petroleum refineries at Skawina, Stanisławów, Nadwórna and moreover at Klosterneuburg near Vienna. The report of a timber firm's bankruptcy arose because EISIG GRIFFEL HAS A BROTHER, A WELL-KNOWN TIMBER INDUSTRIALIST AT BOLECHÓW NEAR STANISŁAWÓW, THANKS TO WHOM HE HAD GREATER CREDIT. The number of those harmed is very great. The liabilities of the bankrupt firm amount exactly to 1,332,000 dollars.
*bankruptcy · the two brothers*

**26 July 1930 — Chwila, year 12, no. 4071 (26 July 1930)**  
CLARIFICATION. Concerning the report of the bankruptcy of the Griffels' oil firm in Stanisławów, we are asked to correct it to the effect that IT DOES NOT CONCERN THE GRIFFEL BROTHERS, BUT ONLY THE FIRM EISIG CHAIM GRIFFEL AND SON.
*bankruptcy · correction*

**30 July 1930 — Chwila, Thursday 31 July 1930, No. 4076**  
AROUND THE BANKRUPTCY OF THE FIRM E. CH. GRIFFEL. Lwów, 30 July. The failures of two great firms are lately a lively topic in Polish economic circles. One is the bankruptcy of the great timber firm Holzindustriegesellschaft A.G. in Bytom, which was in close contact with the Polish timber industry; the other is the insolvency of the well-known oil industrialist and owner of the firm E. Ch. Griffel and Son in Stanisławów, or rather the owner of petroleum refineries at Skawina, Stanisławów and Nadwórna and co-owner of the refinery at Korneuburg in Lower Austria. The declaration of insolvency by this firm took the interested economic circles utterly by surprise, for the firm passed as financially strong and resting on solid foundations. No wonder, then, that it enjoyed great credit interest. IN STANISŁAWÓW, WE ARE TOLD, THE GRIFFEL FIRM PASSED FOR ONE IN WHICH THE PLACING OF CAPITAL WAS SAFER THAN IN THE GREAT BANKS. Holders both of larger capital and of quite small sums hurried, and even competed, to have Griffel accept their savings. When the declaration of insolvency became an accomplished fact, real panic broke out among the wide circles of creditors, who come from the most varied strata. THE GRIFFEL FAMILY LEFT STANISŁAWÓW SEVERAL DAYS BEFORE THE BANKRUPTCY PETITION WAS FILED IN COURT, AND THIS GAVE RISE TO ALL MANNER OF COMMENT. Among the creditors we find the names of high state officials, advocates, doctors, bankers and not least poor widows and orphans, and even, it is said, market-stall and street traders. Among those harmed the first place in the country is taken by the larger banks, to the sum of about 120 thousand dollars, several oil firms (among them Standard Oil Company and Standard Nobel) for approximately a similar sum, then three great Romanian oil firms seated in Vienna, a number of considerable industrialists in Poland and Austria, and so on. In general the liabilities of the Griffel firm amount to about 1,400 thousand dollars, against assets estimated at about 700,000 dollars. The failed firm is said to have offered its creditors 50 per cent. From another quarter rumours have appeared that criminal proceedings for fraudulent bankruptcy have been initiated against the firm.
*bankruptcy · the crash · the family left town*

**29 August 1930 — Chwila, Thursday 30 August 1930, No. 4106**  
ECHOES OF THE FAILURE OF THE FIRM "E. GRIFFEL". Kraków, 29 August. Yesterday the first hearing was fixed at the District Court in Kraków in the composition proceedings of the firm E. Ch. Griffel, which at the end of July declared insolvency. The court appointed as composition commissioner the district judge Mr Jaworski and as administrator of the estate Dr Józef Woźniakowski. Creditors had the right to file claims up to 15 August. The active estate given by the debtors amounts in total to 6,424,000 złoty, including 50 per cent of the Korneuburg A.G. refinery, Vienna (3,020,000 zł). Since tax arrears amount in total to 571,244.49 zł, the net estate available for distribution is 5,852,755.51 zł. The debts shown in the Griffels' application total 11,073,628.90 zł. The statement of the active estate is as follows: 50 per cent of the Korneuburg A.G. refinery, Vienna — 3,020,000 zł; the Skawina refinery — 890,000 zł; the Stanisławów refinery — 666,000; 50 per cent of Wierbiąż — 225,000; the "Babeta" oil shaft at Dźwiniacz — 250,000; oil terrains in the Stanisławów and Jasło (Piętkowa) districts and the shallow shaft "Cecylia" at Pasieczna, gross shares together — 350,000; 50 per cent of the wax mine at Dźwiniacz — 180,000; 50 per cent of real property at Stanisławów — 89,000; stocks at Skawina, Stanisławów and in Romania — 514,000; and finally 40 tank wagons — 214,000. Altogether the active estate amounts to 6,424,000 zł. Under the draft composition the debtors offer 50 per cent of capital with interest to the day of the opening of proceedings, payable in 18 monthly instalments beginning 1 February 1931.
*bankruptcy · the estate itemised*

**23 August 1930 — Chwila, year 12, no. 4100 (24 August 1930)**  
ECHOES OF THE BANKRUPTCY OF THE GRIFFEL FIRM. Lwów, 23 August. The bankruptcy of the Griffel firm of Stanisławów has brought many merchants who placed their capital there to destitution. Now, however — as is reported — the administrators of the composition estate of the bankrupt Griffel firm have concluded negotiations with representatives of "Standard Nobel", which is taking over the firm's immovable property. "Standard", which will run the institutions of the bankrupt firm, is to satisfy the claims of those harmed successively.
*bankruptcy · Standard Nobel*

**30 August 1930 — Ilustrowany Express Wieczorny, year 1, no. 96 (31 August 1930)**  
ULTIMATUM OF THE BANKRUPTCY ESTATE TO GRIFFEL. CREDITORS THREATEN BANKRUPTCY PROCEEDINGS. Stanisławów, 30 August. Yesterday at 7 in the evening a meeting of Griffel's creditors was held in the "Jad Charuzim" hall under the chairmanship of the local advocate Dr Salz. The meeting unanimously adopted a resolution calling on the creditors' committee to summon Griffel to declare, by 8 September at the latest, whether he places his entire property at the disposal of the bankruptcy estate. Within three days of that declaration a further meeting of creditors is to be convened, at which the committee is to report the concrete results of negotiations with Griffel. Otherwise they will file for bankruptcy. The creditors have no confidence in individual members of the creditors' committee; the Stanisławów advocate Dr Salz was therefore elected chairman of the committee. Vienna, 30 August. The administrator of Griffel's bankruptcy estate, Dr Woźniak, is staying here. Rumours have spread in this connection that Griffel was to have covered the claims of the branches of the G.K.O. and the Bank of Poland in Stanisławów.

Ch. Griffel in liquidation". The managers of that company are to be appointed by the creditors, and out of the income or property of the company the creditors are to be satisfied within two years to the extent of 50 per cent of their claims, with the proviso that creditors in a critical financial position will receive their 50 per cent earlier than the others. MRS IDA GRIFFEL, WIDOW OF THE LATE CH. GRIFFEL, is also to join this composition in her own name and as guardian of her minor children, to whom the refinery concessions have passed. These heirs are to receive about 10 per cent of the property of the said company. The above plan has been submitted partly in agreement with the heirs of the late Mr Griffel, but it is not certain whether they, or the guardianship or composition court, will approve such a settlement. For the time being none of the non-privileged creditors has received any money.

I do not know, perhaps such facts did occur, but that proves nothing, because the point is that Mr "Argus" does not state for what timber those prices were obtained — for what kind of wood and what dimensions — and without that the figures cannot be compared. **19 June 1932 — Tajny Detektyw, year II, no. 25, 19 June 1932**  
A MERCHANT TURNS DETECTIVE. Lwów (from our correspondent). Bolechów, a small town in the Subcarpathian region, is a centre of the timber industry. Besides sawmills there are export firms sending timber to every country in the world. Even before the war the export firm "LEIZOR GRIFFEL" was known on international markets; SINCE THE FATHER'S DEATH IT IS RUN BY HIS SON SZAJA. The Griffel firm is in commercial contact with France and even has its own representative in Paris, a certain Zimmermann, who settles supply contracts on the spot and sends the money in the form of cheques on Paris to Griffel at Bolechów. In May Zimmermann carried out a transaction in Paris on Griffel's behalf for 14,000 francs. He sent two cheques by letter, one for 10,000 francs and the other for 4,000. And a strange thing happened: one of those letters, containing the cheque for 4,000 francs, went missing. Griffel telephoned Paris and established beyond doubt that the cheque had been sent. Full of anxiety he went to Lwów, at random, trying his luck. He went first to the "black exchange", reasoning that if someone had stolen the cheque at the post office he had either tried to cash it or had already cashed it there. The dark little Stanisław street, just off Lwów's main artery, is full of suspicious, distrustful figures willing to earn a few złoty on an exchange. Griffel, who often had larger amounts of currency to change himself, was known among these open-air "bankers". The rounds began: "Have you seen a cheque for 4,000 francs?" And — astonishingly — one was actually found to whom a stranger had offered exactly such a cheque the day before. "Yes," he told Griffel, "yesterday a stranger came to me with a cheque for 4,000 francs. I have not bought it yet. But he is to come again today. Wait, Mr Griffel. He will be here shortly." The scene was cleverly arranged: the money-changer was to be the middleman and the supposed buyer none other than Griffel himself. In this way the cheque returned to its lawful owner, and the police found one more post-office "mouse". The arrested man, Emil Jaworski, a post-office clerk of the ninth service grade, comes from Halicz. He is married, lives in Lwów at ul. Paulinów 12, formerly worked in the Directorate and latterly in the newspaper section at the main station. Where did he get the cheque? A tangled story. "I was," says Jaworski, "the day before yesterday in the Venetian café. I was playing dominoes. But a party got up for poker. I wanted to win something. I sat down at the table with 45 groszy, and when I got up I was 700 złoty in pocket. One of the players, the merchant Pępniakiewicz, had no cash. He handed me a cheque for 4,000 francs, signed it on the back and told me I could cash it in the morning wherever I liked. But neither at Gruess's nor at Schuetz and Chajes's would they take it. I came to the black market and here I was arrested. I do not know what for. I am innocent." The police, however, were not trusting. While one of the commissioners conducted the first interrogation, detectives searched the arrested man's home. The results were sensational. In the post-office clerk's house they found a whole store of opened foreign letters, a considerable collection of stamps torn from envelopes, a forged military book in which private first class Emil Jaworski had added a school-leaving certificate under "studies", and a second forged identity card of the Legionaries' Union in which the same private had promoted himself to major of the general staff in retirement, and so on. Now nobody believed his earlier evidence any longer.
*crime story · timber export · the lighter side*

**19 June 1932 — Tajny Detektyw, year II, no. 25, 19 June 1932 (continued)**  
A MERCHANT TURNS DETECTIVE (conclusion). But Jaworski did not lose his nerve for a moment. "The cheque," he said, "I really did not win at cards, but neither did I steal it. I have never stolen any foreign letters at all." — "Then by what miracle did that cheque come into your hands?" they asked at the police station. — "I work together with a certain Marjan Fuglewicz. He gave me the cheque, telling me to sell it for whatever I could get. At the same time he gave me a bundle of foreign letters and asked me to destroy them. I wanted to earn a little, so although I guessed that the cheque and the letters came from theft, I pretended not to be surprised." Fuglewicz was brought in. Jaworski told him to his face that he had had the cheque and the letters from him, but Fuglewicz denied it. A detailed examination began, in which the same method was repeated: while Fuglewicz was giving evidence in the investigation department, detectives carried out a thorough search of his flat at ul. św. Kingi 10. LOVE AND DEBTS. They found a notebook in which Fuglewicz recorded his debts and their repayment dates, and also a remarkably interesting diary. Fuglewicz was in love. Love, which for many becomes a spur to hard and honest work, became for him the reason for turning to crime. He was a frivolous man. He liked to enjoy himself. The modest salary of a clerk of the eleventh category did not suffice for his extravagant needs. So he sank into ever greater and more pressing debts. His fiancée knew how he lived. Sensible and more balanced, she told him outright that she would never marry him until he paid his debts. Looking for sources of income, Fuglewicz reached for … other people's letters. Both accomplices found themselves under lock and key. ONLY GRIFFEL TELLS THE STORY OF HIS SUCCESS WITH PRIDE. He was helped, though, rather by chance, which is often the most brilliant detective of all. Griffel, who had considered his cheque as good as lost, in the course of a single day not only managed to recover it but personally caught the perpetrator of systematic thefts that were endangering the good name of our post office.
*crime story · the lighter side*

**8 November 1932 — Chwila, year 14, no. 4895 (9 November 1932)**  
THE CREDITORS OF THE FIRM E. CH. GRIFFEL AGAINST THE ADMINISTRATOR OF THE COMPOSITION ESTATE. Lwów, 8 November. Much is said and written lately about the administrations of bankruptcy and composition estates, whose administrative costs swallow such great sums that they make the whole essence of composition proceedings illusory. A situation arises which is in the interest neither of creditors nor of debtors. It appears particularly glaringly in the case of the administration described below. Our readers will doubtless remember the impression made in its time by the loud bankruptcy of the firm E. Griffel and Co. at Stanisławów, Skawina and Korneuburg. As is known, the failure was declared before the court in Kraków, which appointed as administrator of the composition estate the advocate Dr Woźniakowski. AMONG THE CREDITORS THE GREAT BULK ARE STANISŁAWÓW WIDOWS AND ORPHANS WHO ENTRUSTED THEIR HARD-SCRAPED PENNIES TO GRIFFEL. Now — the creditors inform us — the administrator of the composition estate has so far collected about one million six hundred thousand złoty, while the creditors have received barely about one hundred thousand złoty. In this state of affairs the creditors have addressed to the president of the appeal court in Kraków a complaint demanding the removal of the present administrator. In the complaint, among others the Bank of Poland in Stanisławów, the Joint-Stock Mortgage Bank in Stanisławów and the Municipal Savings Bank in Stanisławów state that "on account of the claims filed they have not received a penny despite the passing of two years from the date of the opening of composition proceedings". The complaint also bears the signatures of the Banking and Storage House in Stanisławów, the petroleum refinery at Krechowice, THE FIRM LEIZOR GRIFFEL, and others. Independently of this, the consul of the Republic at Moravská Ostrava intervened with the president of the appeal court in Kraków on behalf of several Czechoslovak banks complaining of insufficient protection of creditors' rights. In the coming days the district court in Kraków will consider the complaint, and should the charges in it prove true, Dr Woźniakowski will resign from his post.
*bankruptcy · the widows and orphans · estate scandal*

**31 December 1932 — Chwila, Saturday 31 December 1932, No. 4946**  
JUDGMENT OF THE SUPREME COURT IN THE CASE OF THE OIL FIRMS E. CH. GRIFFEL AND CO. CONCERNING 7,100 SHARES OF THE KORNEUBURGER MINERAL-ÖL-RAFFINERIE. Nowy Dziennik reports: in 1930, after the failure of the Griffel firms, the Vienna industrialists Izydor Adlersberg and Dr Zygfryd Adlersberg brought suit before the District Court in Kraków against the estate of the late EISIG CHAIM GRIFFEL for the surrender of 7,100 shares of one of the largest refineries in Austria, at Korneuburg. The Adlersbergs based their claim on the fact that the late Mr Griffel had deposited those shares in third hands as security for a loan of several tens of thousands of dollars received from them. The District Court in Kraków upheld the claim and ordered the estate to surrender the shares, and the Appeal Court in Kraków confirmed that judgment. On review, however, the Supreme Court altered both lower judgments and dismissed the plaintiffs, ordering them to pay the costs of the dispute. Holding the defendant's review to be justified, the Supreme Court expresses the following legal opinion: an agreement of the kind the late E. Ch. Griffel concluded with the plaintiffs — establishing on the disputed shares a pledge in the plaintiffs' favour of which they could make use only in the event of the debtor's bankruptcy — could not validly create a right of pledge, because that agreement excluded the essential purpose of a pledge under § 447 of the civil code, namely the right to obtain satisfaction from the pledged thing if the obligation is not performed in the appointed time, unless bankruptcy proceedings were opened. And in the event of bankruptcy the agreement secured the plaintiffs an exceedingly advantageous position to the detriment of other creditors. On the strength of this judgment the Adlersbergs' claim will not only not be privileged, but the shares saved from sale, representing half the total share capital of the Korneuburg refinery, will be used for the benefit of the creditors as a whole.
*bankruptcy · supreme court · the shares saved for the creditors*

**22 October 1933 — Głos Podhala, year V, Nowy Sącz, 22 October 1933, No. 43**  
NEWS FROM PODHALE […] MUSZYNA. THE NATIONAL LOAN. The local citizens' Committee for the National Loan, elected on 19 September this year — consisting of mayor Antoni Jurczak as chairman, judge Dr Zygmunt Syguliński as deputy chairman, and as members canon J. Gawor, notary A. Rybiański, advocate Dr J. Kohn, J. Wrona, St. Ogrodnik, Porth, Tymeczko, Jędrzejowski and a number of other representatives of the local offices and citizenry — developed energetic action to popularise the meaning and idea of the National Loan among the widest strata of local society. On the importance of the National Loan and the necessity of the Government issuing it, judge Dr Z. Syguliński delivered a speech on 24 September in the market square to a numerous gathering. Thanks to the efforts of the committee members and the civic maturity of local society, the subscription turned out quite well in relation to the unexceptional material position of the inhabitants of a mountain town such as Muszyna. In particular the local industrialists, merchants, property owners and farmers subscribed 7,000 zł, the commune 1,000 zł, state and communal employees and the free professions 26,050 zł, the Stefczyk Fund 350 zł, the Voluntary Fire Brigade, Rifle Union, Society of Friends of the Rifle Union and the Union of Women's Civic Work together 250 zł, pensioners 3,100 zł, THE FIRM ZYGMUNT GRIFFEL TOGETHER WITH ITS CLERKS 2,500 zł.

**4 November 1933 — Gazeta Lwowska, No. 310, Friday 10 November 1933**  
VISIT OF POLISH TIMBER INDUSTRIALISTS TO GDAŃSK. On Saturday the 4th of this month representatives of the timber industrialists of Poland came to Gdańsk at the invitation of the Port Council, headed by K. Ostrowski, president of the Supreme Council of Timber Associations in Poland, and vice-president A. Monitz. The delegation also included representatives of the timber industry and trade from Lwów, Warsaw, Kraków and Vilna: Mr Ulam, vice-president of the Syndicate of Timber Interests in Lwów, GRIFFEL, VICE-PRESIDENT OF THE SAME SYNDICATE, Dr Wistreich, president of the Association of Timber Industry and Trade in Kraków, and others. The guests paid a visit to the Port Council. At about 10 a.m. a conference was held in the meeting hall under the chairmanship of director Nagórski, with representatives of the Gdańsk Exporters' Union and Gdańsk timber forwarders taking part. The conference was to discuss the general conditions of export and the costs of transhipment and storage of timber in the port of Gdańsk. Those costs have fallen very considerably in the last few years, a fall further increased by the reduction of the port tariff from 1 November. At 1.30 p.m. a luncheon was held at the invitation of the Port Council with the president of the Port Council, representatives of Poland and Gdańsk, both directors and senior officials of the Port Council. In the afternoon conferences took place between the delegates of the Polish industrialists and representatives of the local timber organisations. At 8 in the evening a dinner was given in the guests' honour by the Union of Gdańsk Timber Exporters.

**19 November 1933 — Chwila, year 15, no. 5266 (19 November 1933)**  
WILL THE CREDITORS OF THE FIRM E. CH. GRIFFEL EVER GET THEIR MONEY? The papers in the case take the longest road from Kraków to Warsaw. From Stanisławów it is reported: in July of this year three years passed since the opening of composition proceedings over the estate of the firm E. Ch. Griffel in Stanisławów. We have written before that as a result of this bankruptcy the poorest Jewish families of Stanisławów and the district suffered, having in blind confidence lent the owner of that firm their hard-earned money. These people were deprived of their livelihood and found themselves facing extreme destitution. At first it was hoped that the debtors and the administration of the composition estate would make every effort to return the money at least to the poorest, and that the court through the composition commissioner would press in that direction. Alas, these hopes were disappointed. The composition proceedings are now in their fourth year and the creditors are still waiting patiently. The costs of the estate's administration and of maintaining the debtors' families have swallowed enormous sums. The former administrator Dr Woźniakowski collected for the estate over a million złoty, and of that sum the creditors received about 100,000 zł, that is barely some 5 per cent. After the appointment of a new administrator and composition commissioner the creditors expected a change for the better. The matter, however, grew worse, because since then the creditors receive nothing at all, while the administrator and the debtors conduct themselves as though they had already settled all their debts, taking no account of the creditors' right, through their representatives, to be informed of the state of the estate and to influence how it is run. The costs of administration remain enormous. The administrator and the debtors travel constantly on the estate's business and at its expense, and are rarely in Kraków — for which they of course receive daily allowances (the administrator, it is said, 100 zł a day, besides his fee). In Kraków a great estate office has been set up with a whole staff of clerks, and THE DEBTOR'S SONS RECEIVE HIGH SALARIES AS CLERKS OF THE ESTATE. For them, then, the crisis does not exist; indeed they are better off than before the bankruptcy, and they live at the expense of the poor creditors' money. Several employees still engaged for the estate at the refinery in Stanisławów and at the mines in Dźwiniacz have not been paid their wages for months. Besides this, many former employees are conducting lawsuits against the estate which needlessly burden it with legal costs, especially as the debtors have made it their motto to appeal against every judgment, solely in order to drag the case out. The appeal court in Kraków did not confirm the draft composition, allowing the appeals lodged. The matter should now be decided by the Supreme Court, but the papers have still not left Kraków, and the case is already in its fourth year.
*bankruptcy · estate scandal · the sons on salaries*

**18 January 1934 — Chwila, year 16, no. 5325 (19 January 1934)**  
FROM THE COURT ROOM. THE AFFAIR OF THE FIRM E. CH. GRIFFEL. THE TRUSTEE DR SKĄPSKI HAS RESIGNED HIS MANDATE. Lwów, 18 January. As reported from Kraków, the legal representative of the firm E. Griffel of Stanisławów, Skawina and Korneuburg in Austria, advocate Dr Skąpski, resigned his mandate a few days ago. The fact caused some impression among the creditors of that firm, whose case has lain for years in the Kraków court. The papers have still not gone from the Kraków court to the Supreme Court in Warsaw, which is finally to decide the question of composition or bankruptcy. The small creditors, mostly Stanisławów widows and orphans whose claims do not exceed a thousand dollars, cannot, despite the passage of a long time, obtain satisfaction. This causes understandable bitterness among these unfortunates, who in blind confidence deposited their hard-scraped pennies. In the near future the epilogue of the firm's insolvency is to be played out before the criminal tribunal in Stanisławów.
*bankruptcy · the widows and orphans*

**11 July 1934 — Nowy Czas, No. 174, 11 July 1934**  
A PLATINUM BROOCH WITH A PEARL. THE TAILOR'S MUDDY EXPLANATIONS. From Lwów it is reported: a brooch with a pearl in a platinum setting, worth over 4,000 zł, disappeared mysteriously from the flat of ZYGMUNT GRIFFEL, timber industrialist, resident at ul. Matejki 8. The Griffels suspected nobody of the theft and did not inform the police, but turned to a private detective, the retired police sergeant Riedel, asking him to track down the thief and offering a reward of 300 zł. Mr Riedel notified above all every pawnshop in Lwów of the theft. Several months passed. A clerk of the Mons Pius pawnshop, Mr Dąbrowski, on receiving such a notice, remembered that somebody had pawned a similar brooch in that establishment, and merely waited for the person to come and pay the interest, or possibly redeem it, so as to have him arrested. Two days ago the supposed owner of the brooch appeared and wanted to redeem it. As there was no constable nearby and the clerk did not wish to attract the person's attention, he told him to come back in two days and then he could redeem it. Yesterday the man came, and Mr Dąbrowski got in touch with the investigation department, which sent a detective; he arrested the stranger at the moment when he wanted to redeem the brooch stolen from the Griffels. Taken to the investigation department, he gave his name as Fryderyk Benedykt Diener, aged 26, living at ul. Szpitalna 23, a tailor by trade, latterly running a sewing workshop at ul. Skarbowa 5. Under examination Diener defended himself by saying that while at the Griffels' house, where he had taken trousers to press, he had found the brooch on the stairs; he did not know whose it was, and wanting to find out what it was worth, pawned it at the Mons Pius. This defence is untrue. It turned out that the Griffels had asked Diener whether he had found a brooch in the trousers, and he had then answered no. Diener probably stole the brooch during his visit to the Griffels. He was placed in police custody.
*theft · the lighter side*

**27 October 1934 — Nasz Głos, 27 October 1934, no. 9, year 1 (editorial office: Kazimierzowska 10, the Weidenfeld printing house; publishers Jakób Lautmann and Pinkas Weissbach; responsible editor Izak Kern)**  
THE MATTER OF THE FIRM GRIFFEL. In issue 3 of our weekly we gave the present stage of the Griffel estate and the composition of the liquidation committee. We now learn that Dr Arnold, formerly the Griffels' legal representative during Dr Woźniakowski's administration, has lodged an appeal to the court in Kraków against the appointment of that committee by Dr Rychlewski. In consequence the composition of the liquidation committee has partly changed, and those members appointed in 1932 by Dr Woźniakowski have returned, except for the latter himself and Dr Landau, who renounced their mandates. As we see from this, THE GRIFFEL ESTATE HAS BECOME AN OPERATING FIELD FOR KRAKÓW ADVOCATES, who outdo one another in creating ever new legal situations, from which of course the creditors suffer most, having no influence at all on the administration of the estate. And every such change swallows enormous sums. By way of example only, we state that the members of the liquidation committee appointed by Dr Woźniakowski in 1932 drew 200 zł for every sitting — and it need hardly be added that such sittings were held quite often. What sums this committee took is shown by the fact that sittings were often held for three days running, so that each member received 600 zł for that time; and as there were 11 members, such a sitting cost 6,600 zł (from Stanisławów travelled director Ziobrowski and M. L. Scherzer). As later transpired, these sittings were pointless, for nothing was achieved, and the appeal court in Kraków ruled that the calling into being of the liquidation committee in 1932 was altogether inadmissible, since the composition was not yet legally valid. But what of it? The liquidation committee took the money and nobody was called to account for it. The day before yesterday a sitting of the new liquidation committee was held in Kraków, at which the former administrator and present chairman of the committee Dr Rychlewski announced that (continued on page 5)
*bankruptcy · estate scandal · the lawyers*

**27 October 1934 — Nasz Głos, 27 October 1934, no. 9 (continued from page 3)**  
… that during his term of office, that is within not quite two years, he collected 1,100,000 złoty, which he spent, and demands a further payment of about 40,000 zł. Of that sum the creditors received almost nothing, and the estate's liabilities decreased negligibly. On what, then, was this money spent? For the present Dr Rychlewski has not submitted accounts to the committee. We hope, however, that the committee members will see to it, and will also inform the creditors, so that they may at least know how their money was managed. Under the composition the debtors are to transfer their whole property to the liquidation committee, which they have so far not done; we are informed that the present liquidation committee has summoned the debtors to do so within 30 days, failing which bankruptcy proceedings will be opened. In the next issue we shall deal with the matter of the local refinery, leased by Messrs Taub and Petranker under the firm "Benzonafta", and with the matter of the employees of the Griffel estate.
*bankruptcy · estate scandal*

**3 November 1934 — Nasz Głos, 3 November 1934, no. 10, year 1**  
AROUND THE GRIFFEL AFFAIR. After the opening of composition proceedings over the property of the firm Griffel in 1930, the then administrator Dr Woźniakowski leased the refineries at Stanisławów and Skawina to the firm "Limanowa" for about 10,000 dollars a month. Since an oil cartel existed at that time, "Limanowa" did not work those refineries but took the quota allotted to the Griffel firm. The oil cartel existed until 1 April 1933, and after its dissolution the estate administration began negotiations with the firm Taub and Petranker for the lease of the local refinery. These negotiations lasted several months; enormous sums were spent on the travelling costs of the composition administrator Dr Rychlewski, of the Griffels and of the estate's clerks, who travelled countless times from Kraków to Lwów and Stanisławów. Mr Scherzer also took part as a member of the administrator's advisory council and as an intermediary. In December 1933 the negotiations were concluded and a partnership agreement was drawn up between the Griffel estate and the firm Taub and Petranker. The estate's share in the partnership is 67 per cent and the firm Taub and Petranker's 33 per cent. In this agreement the partners undertook TO PAY THE WIDOW OF THE LATE E. CH. GRIFFEL, IDA GRIFFEL, 1,500 zł A MONTH for granting the partnership the concession, which after her husband's death passed to the widow and the minor children. Besides this the partnership is to pay 2,000 zł a month towards tax arrears. (We are informed that after paying five monthly instalments the partnership stopped paying.) Outwardly the partnership looks like this: on 11 December 1933 the firm "BENZONAFTA", limited liability company in Stanisławów, was entered in the commercial register of the local court (L. 103/1) — processing of crude oil and trade in the products obtained from it. Share capital 20,000 zł in cash. As managers were appointed Isak Rosenthal and Zacharjasz Tropper, who are entitled to sign for the firm. Duration of the company to the end of March 1937. On 5 March 1934 it was entered in the register (103/2) that by resolution of the general meeting of 26 February 1934 the share capital was reduced from 20,000 zł to 10,000 zł. The firm "Benzonafta" processed 100 tank-cars of crude and the remainder of the stocks left in the refinery after their sale to the firm Taub and Petranker in 1932, after which the refinery's operation was temporarily halted. We learn that the books of "Benzonafta" are now to be checked by a member of the liquidation committee, director Ziobrowski. For what purpose the firm "Benzonafta" was created, and its managers appointed — Isak Rosenthal (an insurance agent) and Zacharjasz Tropper (a bookkeeper of the firm Taub and Petranker) — is a riddle to the creditors, which in their interest we shall try to solve. (To be continued.)
*bankruptcy · estate scandal · the widow’s 1,500 a month*

**10 November 1934 — Nasz Głos, 10 November 1934, no. 11, year 1**  
AROUND THE GRIFFEL AFFAIR III. WHAT HAVE THE LOCAL MEMBERS OF THE LIQUIDATION COMMITTEE DONE FOR THE STANISŁAWÓW CREDITORS? All matters connected with the administration of the Griffel estate are covered by a mist of secrecy, and the creditors, who ought to know of the more important property transactions, learn everything post factum, when it is too late. After two years of Dr Rychlewski's administration it emerged that he had collected over a million złoty, of which the creditors perhaps received 1 per cent. So it was too with the creation of the firm "Benzonafta" — a transaction concerning the local refinery, concluded between the Griffel estate and the firm Taub and Petranker, of which the majority of creditors knew nothing until now. It was the duty of those gentlemen who at the time of the agreements formally represented the creditors (as members of the administrator's advisory council, now the liquidation committee) and who, living permanently in Stanisławów — director Ziobrowski and M. L. Scherzer — surely had the opportunity to consult the local creditors and tell them what was to happen to the local refinery. IS IT JUST THAT, OUT OF THE MONEY OBTAINED FROM THE SALE OF STOCKS AND THE PROCESSING OF CRUDE FROM THE STANISŁAWÓW REFINERY, THE LOCAL CREDITORS — WHO SUFFERED MOST FROM THE BANKRUPTCY — RECEIVED NOT A PENNY, WHILE MRS IDA GRIFFEL RECEIVED 1,500 zł A MONTH and the rest of the income was spent on enormous and unnecessary costs? What income "Benzonafta" has had so far and how it was used is for the present known only to the interested parties, that is to the partners. The administration of the Griffel estate conducts itself as though it had already satisfied all the creditors and they had no right whatever to the Griffels' property; and the local members of the former advisory council and present liquidation committee, who are informed of everything and do not oppose it, act to the creditors' harm. Whenever a creditor turned to these members for information about the state of the composition estate, or asked them to intervene with the administrator to obtain even a small part of his claim, he received the answer that "there is a terrible mess in the administration of the estate, terrible villainies go on there, they throw enormous sums at unnecessary lawsuits" and similar opinions. It is strange, however, that these committee members, despite such exact and "flattering" information about the administration, draw no consequences from it and neither resign their mandates nor inform the judicial supervisory authorities. We note that creditors have been receiving such information for several years now, and these gentlemen keep announcing that they will resign, but have somehow not put these "threats" into effect. Presumably they are concerned to defend the interests of the poor creditors! In the name of the injured local creditors we call on the gentlemen of the liquidation committee, citizens of our town, who from almost the very beginning have cooperated with the administration, to render an account of their activities and to tell the local creditors how they defended their interests and what they achieved for them. They may do so at a public meeting of creditors or through the press, but it is high time to break the silence and inform the creditors of the present legal and property state of the estate, and whether any possibility still exists of saving even part of the property for the creditors. We know that the debtors and the administration have an interest in the composition lasting as long as possible, because they profit by it, even though the value of the property steadily diminishes, so that in time nothing will be left for the creditors.
*bankruptcy · estate scandal · the local creditors got nothing*

**24 November 1934 — Nasz Głos, 24 November 1934, no. 13, year 1**  
AROUND THE GRIFFEL AFFAIR IV. THE LOT OF THE FIRM'S EMPLOYEES. As we have written in previous articles, the present chairman of the liquidation committee and former composition administrator Dr Rychlewski collected during his term (from December 1932) over a million złoty, out of which he first of all covered his own remuneration and expenses. Under the act of 1933 the maximum monthly remuneration of a composition administrator is 3,000 zł, and as that sum is too small for Dr Rychlewski he found another way: while staying "on estate business" outside Kraków he receives allowances of 100 zł a day — and it need hardly be added that he therefore spends most of his time outside Kraków, always finding a reason for it. The administrator's claims are indeed privileged, but there are other claims which by law enjoy priority, namely the wages of the estate's employees. But the administration of the Griffel estate finds a way round the law in this as in other matters. AT THE REFINERY IN STANISŁAWÓW AND AT THE ESTATE'S MINES IN DŹWINIACZ SEVERAL EMPLOYEES ARE ENGAGED TO WHOM THE ADMINISTRATION PAYS NO MONTHLY WAGES AT ALL. THE EMPLOYEES IN STANISŁAWÓW ARE OWED 13 MONTHS' WAGES, AND THOSE IN DŹWINIACZ 23! Instead of money Dr Rychlewski sends them from time to time a consoling letter expressing the "hope of transferring more considerable advances in the coming days" and drawing attention to "the necessity of understanding the difficult situation in which the composition estate now finds itself" and similar phrases. Yes, counsellor! The estate's situation is indeed difficult, if three employees at Dźwiniacz are not paid wages amounting together to 260 zł a month, while one draws 3,000 zł oneself. It is curious that all the employees' requests and complaints to the labour inspector, to the district office at Nadwórna, and even to the president of the district court in Kraków remain without effect, and the administration neither pays nor dismisses. Nothing remains for the employees but to sue the estate for their wages. But to conduct a lawsuit with the Griffel estate is no easy thing, for it lasts years, especially as the papers go from the court in Kraków for the Griffels to be heard, and lie there for months undealt with. There have been cases where employees voluntarily reduced their claims by even half rather than sue the estate, but the administration prefers to pay legal costs which often exceed the value of the claim, solely in order to drag the matter out. By way of example we cite the case of two former employees who repeatedly and vainly asked for a settlement, declaring themselves ready to reduce their claims considerably, and were finally obliged to bring an action in the district court at Sołotwina. And now begins the curious course of a lawsuit in which the estate's counsel uses every possible means to prolong the dispute — and of course to increase his fee correspondingly. At the first hearing the Griffels' counsel objected that the Sołotwina court lacked jurisdiction, and the judge upheld the objection. The plaintiffs appealed to the district court in Stanisławów, which allowed the appeal and ordered the Griffel estate to pay the costs. The case therefore returned to the court at Sołotwina. Now the estate applied to be granted poor person's rights in the dispute, since it "possesses no property, for the liabilities considerably exceed the assets". When poor person's rights were refused, the Griffels' counsel in Kraków (Dr Arnold) appealed to the district court in Stanisławów, stating that "the defendant estate possesses no property, since the whole property has been handed over to the creditors' committee to cover the estate's debts" — this was in 1932, and as is known the property has to this day still not been handed over. The district court did not allow that appeal either, and finally the court at Sołotwina gave judgment (II C 245/31 of 18 May 1932) granting the plaintiffs their claims together with 10 per cent interest and the costs of the dispute. Against that judgment the Griffels again appealed to the district court in Stanisławów, objecting that the defendant — the curator of the estate DAWID MENDEL GRIFFEL — had not been heard. (The said person, despite repeated summonses, did not appear at the hearing in Kraków.) The district court rejected the appeal and the plaintiffs at last had a valid judgment. But even then the administration, despite the employees' pleas, would not pay them, and only when they obtained execution against the immovable property and the inventory of the mine — part of which was sold for a song at auction — were they paid what was due. As we see, this lawsuit ran three times in the district court at Sołotwina and three times in the district court at Stanisławów on appeal, and each time the estate was ordered to pay costs. Moreover in this small case three counsel were engaged on the side of the Griffel estate — one in Kraków (who directed the whole course of the matter), a second at Sołotwina and a third at Stanisławów; as many were engaged for the plaintiffs, and through losing the case the administration had to pay all six advocates. All this at the expense of the creditors, who instead of the return of their lent money receive a statement of the costs of administering the estate. The estate conducts several dozen such lawsuits with employees, and only in rare cases does it win. Are these lawsuits known to the judicial supervisory authorities in Kraków, and why are they tolerated?
*bankruptcy · estate scandal · unpaid workers*

**1 December 1934 — Nasz Głos, 1 December 1934, no. 14, year 1**  
THE GRIFFEL ESTATE … WITHOUT AN ADMINISTRATION! TWO LETTERS. What the administration of the Griffel estate now looks like is shown by the two letters below, of the same date. To the employees' complaint addressed to the President of the District Court in Kraków that FOR 23 MONTHS THEY HAVE NOT RECEIVED THEIR WAGES, they received the following: "District Court in Kraków, Civil Section I, 20 November 1934, I. Sa 1925/30. In the composition matter of the Griffels' estate, notice is given that the composition proceedings were concluded by our decision of 30 June 1934, and that the Griffels' property has been taken into administration by the liquidation committee under the composition concluded between the creditors and the debtor, whose chairman is the advocate Dr Bolesław Rychlewski in Kraków, Straszewskiego 26. All claims should therefore be addressed to the said chairman. (signed) Władysław Robaczewski" — the hitherto composition commissioner of the Griffel estate, the editors note. An employee of the local refinery, to whom the estate has not paid wages for 13 months, applied through a local advocate to Dr Rychlewski for settlement of his claim. That advocate received a letter from Dr Rychlewski dated 20 November 1934 — THE SAME DATE as the court's letter above — in which he writes, among other arguments: "As for the matter itself, allow me first of all to inform my colleague that all your client's claims can now be addressed only directly to the firm Griffel, since I was only the composition administrator of the firm, and have now even ceased to be that. The composition proceedings ended on 30 June this year, on which day I was released from my duties and received from this court a full discharge as to the accounts and the whole of the financial management." Dr Rychlewski has indeed ceased to be composition administrator, but unfortunately he is chairman of the liquidation committee, which in his "modesty" he does not even mention in the above letter. WHO IS NOW TO DECIDE TO WHOM CLAIMS SHOULD BE ADDRESSED, when the Kraków district court directs them to Dr Rychlewski and he in turn "directly to the Griffels"? Who, then, now represents the Griffel estate? Which of the two letters is authoritative? The only conclusion — anarchy reigns in the administration of the Griffel estate.
*bankruptcy · estate scandal · 23 months unpaid*

**13 December 1934 — Chwila, evening edition, year 1, no. 86 (13 December 1934)**  
TWO CITIZENS SUFFERED 3,200 zł OF DAMAGE. Lwów, 13 December. On the night of Wednesday to Thursday unknown perpetrators broke into the flat of ZYGMUNT GRIFFEL at ul. Matejki 8 by cutting out a door panel. Although the household were in the flat, deep in sleep, the burglars went about noiselessly and woke nobody. They took one man's fur coat and a wallet containing 500 zł in cash. The total damage is 2,500 zł. In the second case unknown perpetrators broke into the home of Otto Miller (Podleskiego 9), stealing clothing worth 700 zł. The police today began a search for the perpetrators of both burglaries.
*burglary*

**20 June 1935 — Chwila, evening edition, year II, no. 247 (20 June 1935)**  
ECHOES OF THE BANKRUPTCY OF THE FIRM GRIFFEL. A SENSATIONAL TRIAL BEFORE THE STANISŁAWÓW COURT. From Stanisławów it is reported: in the first days of July a sensational trial will be held before the Stanisławów court, the epilogue of the bankruptcy of the family GRIFFEL, in their time well-known oil magnates of eastern Little Poland. Our readers will remember how many tears and misfortunes the Griffels' bankruptcy brought with it, how many thousands of people lost their fortunes or savings gathered by the sweat of their brow. Well-known banking institutions in Little Poland were also seriously involved in the Griffels' business. THE GRIFFEL OIL UNDERTAKING IN 1930 was an extraordinarily ramified centre of the oil industry, its establishments covering not only several places in Little Poland but abroad, namely Austria and Romania. It comprised: the Skawina Refinery at Skawina, the First Stanisławów Petroleum Factory at Stanisławów, the "Wierbiąż" refinery near Kołomyja, the "Babetta" oil shaft at Dźwiniacz, the "Dobra Nadzieja" mine at Dźwiniacz, and a series of oil terrains at Bitków, Pasieczna, Pniów, Starunia, Babcze, Maniawa, Majdan, Dźwiniacz, Rosulna; 50 per cent of the shares of the Korneuburger Mineralölraffinerie A.G. in Austria; and terrains in Romania. Besides this the Griffels had private property. THE WHOLE PROPERTY OF THE FIRM GRIFFEL REPRESENTED IN 1930 A VALUE OF 7,734,672 zł. Such a considerable undertaking employed in July 1930 more than 30 clerks. The centre of the undertaking was Stanisławów, where THE LATE CHAIM EISIG GRIFFEL AND HIS SON SZULIM GRIFFEL held office. THE AFFAIRS OF THE KORNEUBURG REFINERY WERE HANDLED BY THE SECOND SON, JAKÓB GRIFFEL; THE THIRD SON, MENDEL GRIFFEL, DEALT WITH TECHNICAL MATTERS; AND THE YOUNGEST, OZJASZ HENRYK, WORKED IN HIS FATHER'S OFFICE. The undertaking commanded considerable capital in the years 1917-1922 and in that period acquired a whole series of oil terrains, mines and refineries. After the collapse of the Polish mark in 1924 the firm was already, by 1926, under water — that is, working at a loss. Relying, however, on the profitable refineries at Stanisławów and Skawina, the Griffels not only did not confine themselves to balancing the books but took steps towards an enormous expansion of their undertaking, engaging — for want of sufficient capital of their own — other people's capital. The investments they undertook were enormous, considering the purchase of the "Dobra Nadzieja" mine and entry into the Romanian business and the Korneuburg refinery, where 60,000 dollars were paid out and a further 32,000 dollars were to be paid to start that refinery up. To obtain such great sums the Griffels drew into circulation outside capital, both from banks and from private persons. As cover and security for the money accepted, promissory notes or endorsed remittances were issued, while mortgage security on real property was scrupulously avoided — which made a favourable impression on lenders as to the firm's active financial state and made borrowing easier. These loans were taken at very high interest, 18 to 24 per cent a year, the interest as a rule being paid in advance. Both the high interest paid in advance and the clean mortgage register, and the large trade turnover visible from outside, gave the firm the appearance of a solid and solvent house and a convenient place to make capital fruitful — an appearance the Griffels zealously maintained. When confidence in their own signatures began to waver, they drew into circulation the remittances of other firms incapable of covering their obligations and, taking advantage of local ignorance of those firms' payment standing, put their remittances into circulation, strengthening them with their own endorsement. The organisation of the undertaking itself was conducted so that only those in whose interest it lay could understand the state of its affairs: NO COMMERCIAL BOOKS WERE KEPT AND NO BALANCE SHEETS WERE DRAWN UP AT ALL. In this state of affairs the Griffels filed on 17 July 1930 in the Kraków court an application for composition proceedings over their property, offering creditors 50 per cent of their claims. The passive state of the composition estate at that time amounted to 16,250,953 złoty. Comparing that figure with the active state gives a shortfall of over eight and a half million złoty; the firm could therefore in the summer of 1930 cover at most 50 per cent of its obligations. Then came the crash. The Griffels began to save themselves, being unable to meet their obligations. Unclean business was done right and left. They wanted at all costs to save themselves from ruin, taking no account of the fact that by their careless policy they had made thousands of poor people wretched, whose savings melted in the pockets of dishonest calculators. It came to the point where the prosecutor took an interest. A long and laborious investigation gave concrete results. The Griffels' trial has been fixed for 8, 9 and 10 July. In the dock will sit SCHULIM GRIFFEL, Leizor Weisblum, HENRYK GRIFFEL, Markus Blaustein and Leon Horowitz. Several dozen witnesses have been summoned to the trial, which arouses great interest not only in Stanisławów but throughout Little Poland, and is already the subject of general conversation in our town, where most of those harmed live.
*criminal trial · the four sons named · how the empire worked*

**23 June 1935 — Ostatnie Wiadomości Krakowskie, year V, Kraków, Sunday 23 June 1935, No. 172**  
ECHOES OF THE BANKRUPTCY OF THE FIRM GRIFFEL. In the first days of July a sensational trial will be held before the Stanisławów court, the epilogue of the bankruptcy of the family Griffel, in their time well-known oil magnates of eastern Little Poland. The Griffel oil undertaking was in 1930 an extraordinarily ramified centre of the oil industry, reaching beyond Little Poland and abroad. It comprised the Skawina Refinery at Skawina and much else. The whole property of the firm represented in 1930 a value of 7,734,672 zł. Commanding considerable capital, in 1917-1922 the undertaking acquired a series of oil terrains, mines and refineries, in which it began to invest after 1926. These investments were enormous: the investment in the "Dobra Nadzieja" mine and the starting up of the Korneuburg refinery cost 92,000 dollars. To obtain such sums outside capital was engaged, from banks and from private persons. As cover and security, promissory notes or endorsed remittances were issued, while mortgage security was avoided, which made a favourable impression as to the firm's active financial state and eased borrowing. The loans were taken at very high interest, 18 to 24 per cent a year. Then, like a bolt from a clear sky, came the news of 17 July 1930 that the Griffels had applied for composition proceedings, offering creditors 50 per cent. The passive state was then 16,250,953 zł, giving a shortfall of over eight and a half million. The firm could cover at most 50 per cent of its obligations. Then came the crash. Unclean business was done right and left until the prosecutor took an interest. As a result of the investigation those held responsible are: the brothers SCHULIM AND OZJASZ GRIFFEL, resident in Kraków at Aleja Krasińskiego 20, Leizor Weisblum, Markus Blaustein and Leon Horowitz.
*criminal trial*

**8 July 1935 — Chwila, evening edition, year II, no. 262, Lwów (8 July 1935)**  
THE GRIFFELS BEFORE THE COURT. A GREAT CRIMINAL TRIAL IN STANISŁAWÓW. Stanisławów, 8 July. The Griffel trial, the epilogue of the sensational bankruptcy, began today before the tribunal of the district court in Stanisławów. The tribunal is presided over by the vice-president of the court Dr Cysarz; the prosecutor's chair is taken by prosecutor Gertz. There have also been changes on the defence bench: SZULIM GRIFFEL is defended by advocate Guertler of Lwów; OZJASZ GRIFFEL and the accused Markus Blaustein by Dr Zasławski of Stanisławów; the accused Leizor Weissblum by advocates Dr Landau of Lwów and Dr Darm of Stanisławów. The presiding judge took the personal particulars of the accused, after which the extensive indictment, already known to readers of Chwila, was read. The presiding judge then began the examination of the first of the accused, SZALOM GRIFFEL. The accused does not admit guilt. The presiding judge proceeded to clarify the tangled matter of Major Haller's promissory notes. The accused testifies that in 1922 his father bought oil terrains at Starunia together with Haller for 5,000 dollars. Griffel had 45 per cent of the shares and Haller 55 per cent. For his shares Griffel paid at once in cash, while Haller issued promissory notes. When Griffel wanted to sell the oil terrain, Haller declared that he could not redeem the notes he had issued and asked for an extension. On this matter the accused conferred on his father's behalf. Agreement was reached: the Griffels were to extend Haller's notes, and Haller was to sign accommodation notes for him. The trial continues.
*criminal trial*

**9 July 1935 — Chwila, year 17, no. 5854 (9 July 1935)**  
HOW THE GRIFFELS DEFEND THEMSELVES. Stanisławów, 9 July. The trial proceeding here since yesterday before the adjudicating tribunal of the district criminal court in Stanisławów, under the presidency of vice-president Cysarz — the epilogue of the bankruptcy, loud in its time, of the firm EISIG GRIFFEL AND SONS — arouses enormous interest far beyond the borders of our town. As is known, the bankruptcy of the firm Griffel harmed, besides great banks and firms, HUNDREDS OF THE POOREST JEWISH FAMILIES, WIDOWS AND ORPHANS, CABMEN AND CARTERS, who in confidence in the firm's owners placed their savings, very often the last penny taken from their own mouths. Unhappily things turned out so that those harmed received almost nothing, and at best 10 to 20 per cent. No wonder, then, that the present trial has aroused such enormous interest, all the more as the composition proceedings, transferred to Kraków, were and are unique of their kind and the subject of widely commented conversation in legal and lay circles alike. For some time, and apparently even lately, THE SONS OF EISIG GRIFFEL — against whom the investigation had to be discontinued because he had died in the meantime — employed at the bankruptcy estate, nevertheless drew fees and salaries not at all low. Yesterday in the dock sat the two sons of the late Eisig Griffel, SZULIM AND OZJASZ, together with the firm's former clerks Leisor Weissblum, Markus Blaustein and Leon Horowitz. All alike deny guilt. In particular they deny the charge of having flooded the market with false remittances. The bankruptcy itself they seek to explain by an alleged war with the firm's partner in Korneuburg, which supposedly forced them to suspend payments. All the accused have now been examined. Today the evidentiary proceedings will open. The trial was set down for three days, but it is very doubtful whether it will end tomorrow, as the material is very extensive.
*criminal trial · the widows and orphans*

**9 July 1935 — Chwila, evening edition, year 2, no. 263 (9 July 1935)**  
MOB JUSTICE AGAINST THE GRIFFELS. Stanisławów, 9 July. On the second day of the trial the court proceeded to examine one of the chief witnesses, MAJER HALLER, industrialist and former mayor of Sołotwina, who as a result of the Griffels' speculation became literally a beggar. The witness had traded with the Griffels since 1922 and had bought oil terrains jointly with them. He testifies bitterly that he suffered great losses and was materially ruined altogether. He not only put money into the oil terrains but supplied the Griffels with 5,300 dollars in cash, for which he received promissory notes. He had to put those notes into circulation and then redeem them himself, because the Griffels did not redeem them. "I became a poor man," says Haller before the tribunal, "and they" — pointing at the accused — "are well off and doing well." The Griffels' machinations were various. The witness testifies that, for example, he issued notes for at most 3,000 zł, while the Griffels, having notes signed in blank, raised the amount, paid the stamp duty at the tax office and put them into circulation. "At the moment of signing the notes I never saw additional stamp charges on the blanks." He thought his accommodation notes handed to the Griffels came to at most 5,000 dollars; meanwhile the Griffels raised the sum on their own initiative to about 30,000 dollars and put them into circulation. After Haller's examination came a confrontation with the accused SZULIM GRIFFEL, in which Haller states categorically that it was Szulim Griffel who ran the Griffels' business and was better acquainted with the course of affairs than Haller himself. The accused Weissblum too knew the business perfectly. PRESIDING JUDGE to Szulim Griffel: "Why did you make use of such low and base credit, from the point of view of finance, from these little people, and take money from widows and orphans?" ACCUSED GRIFFEL: "Intermediaries brought us the money." Haller adds that Szulim Griffel, taking new notes, assured him they would be destroyed, and that this did not happen. When Haller learned that his notes had been put into circulation for so great a sum, he knew he was materially ruined. He asked Ozjasz Griffel, if any notes were left, not to circulate them and to return them to him; but Ozjasz told him that all the notes had been put into circulation. The presiding judge then began the examination of the former director of the Stanisławów branch of the Mortgage Bank, Horowitz. The trial continues. — From Stanisławów it is further reported: on Monday at 4.30, after the hearing was adjourned to Tuesday, the Griffel brothers were returning with their defender advocate Guertler of Lwów to the hotel "Union" where they were staying. As they entered the vestibule a young man in a brown suit threw himself on one of the brothers and struck him in the face, then seized a chair standing there and made to hit him with it. Advocate Guertler prevented this, wrenching the chair from the man, who used the confusion to escape. Numerous passers-by gathered by the hotel, discussing this unusual case of summary justice by one of those harmed by the Griffels. In the evening hours a second attack was made, and today another as they walked to the court. [Chwila corrected the following day: the man attacked was SZULIM GRIFFEL, who was pulled from a cab and had his eye blackened.]
*criminal trial · attacked in the street*

**10 July 1935 — Chwila, year 17, no. 5855 (10 July 1935)**  
HOW THE GRIFFELS EARNED … Stanisławów. The second day of the trial was devoted to further witnesses. After Haller the court examined ADOLF HOROWITZ, retired director of the Stanisławów branch of the Mortgage Bank. In May, the director begins, he was approached as director of the Mortgage Bank to advance money against the Griffels' goods, supposedly cylinder oil. The Korneuburg refinery had ordered 200 tonnes of that oil from the Griffels and they had sent 14 tank-cars of it to that refinery. On behalf of his chiefs the accused Weissblum intervened with the witness, asking for the goods to be pledged. Having obtained information about the value of cylinder oil and examined the whole matter, he agreed, on a merchant's reasoning, to pay the Griffels 7,100 dollars, to be recovered after the Korneuburg refinery had redeemed the goods. He therefore ordered the sum paid, acting in good faith. A few days later he received word from Vienna that the Korneuburg refinery had not redeemed the goods. This gave him much to think about; he supposed something was wrong. He was not alarmed, however, since cylinder oil had at that time an established market price. Questioned about the non-redemption, Weissblum explained that it was merely the revenge of the firm Adlersberg of Vienna, with which the Griffels had for some time been in competitive conflict. The accused Szulim Griffel reassured the witness in the same spirit. Despite these assurances the witness wrote to director Adlersberg, a good acquaintance, for an explanation. Adlersberg replied asking for a sample of the oil, which was at that time standing at the Czech border at Bohumín. A clerk was sent from Stanisławów to draw oil from the tank-car and the sample was sent to Vienna, whence after a few days came word that the goods were of little value. The alarmed witness then sent a sample to the experimental station in Warsaw for expert examination. THE RESULT WAS UNEXPECTED: THE TANK-CARS CONTAINED NOT CYLINDER OIL BUT CARRIAGE OIL, THE SO-CALLED "NIEDOBEN", CHEAPER BY THREE AND A HALF DOLLARS THAN CYLINDER OIL. The Mortgage Bank could not therefore realise the oil; it was sold only a year later, and on that transaction with the Griffels about 6,000 dollars were lost. The witness had not sought business with the Griffels, knowing that their promissory-note debts ran to three million dollars. He had believed from the consignment notes that the oil was cylinder oil and had not supposed the Griffels would so deceive him. He added that he regarded the accused Szulim Griffel as the director of his father's business, since the late Eisig Chaim Griffel did not concern himself with business. For the 7,100 dollars paid out the Mortgage Bank received a guarantee from LEISOR GRIFFEL, but it was overlooked that the security was worthless, since instead of the three signatures required by the register only two were given. Next the bookkeeper of the Savings Bank, Maria Biczyńska, testified, after which a break was ordered. At the afternoon hearing REGINA FREILÓWNA, a private clerk, testified. She states that in May 1930 the intermediary Ehrlich came to her offering for discount promissory notes of Efraim Rothblum endorsed by Griffel. Through the same Ehrlich she had once already lent 500 dollars, and now, lending money on Rothblum's notes, she asked whether the firm was good; to which the intermediary declared: "it is first-rate". Meanwhile the Griffels' bankruptcy followed, and Freilówna turned to her advocate in Kraków, who established from the probate papers that the acceptor Rothblum, who was already dead, had left property showing 1,025 zł of assets and 1,058 zł of liabilities. After Rothblum's death — he had owed the Griffels 1,000 dollars — his son-in-law Horowitz began signing notes for his father-in-law. Dawid Feil, brother of the previous witness, the intermediary Pinkas Wagszal and Stefania Mannheim also testified. The hearing was then adjourned. Today about 20 more witnesses will be examined in the morning; in the afternoon the prosecutor and defenders will speak, and the verdict is expected late at night.
*criminal trial · the oil that was not cylinder oil*

**11 July 1935 — Chwila, year 17, no. 5856 (11 July 1935)**  
INCIDENTS AT THE GRIFFEL TRIAL. A SHORT CIRCUIT IN THE COURT BUILDING. Stanisławów, 11 July. We reported that the trial was set down for three days; it turned out, however, that the material could not be exhausted in that time, so the trial is being prolonged. Wednesday's hearing abounded in sensational details, above all the evidence of ZIOBROWSKI, director of the Municipal Communal Savings Bank in Stanisławów, which made a great impression. According to this witness, in 1930 the accused Weissblum came and, producing three promissory notes for 20,500 Swiss francs, asked for credit for the Griffels. Acting in good faith and regarding the Griffels as an excellent firm, he sent the notes for rediscount to the Bank of Poland, placing on them the endorsement of the Municipal Savings Bank. The Bank of Poland, however, refused credit and returned the notes, of which the witness informed Weissblum, who took them back — the savings bank's endorsement having been struck through with a chemical pencil. THE GRIFFELS, HOWEVER, REMOVED THOSE CROSSINGS-OUT AND PUT THE NOTES INTO CIRCULATION IN KRAKÓW, WHERE THEY WERE DISCOUNTED BY THE BANK ZACHODNI. "I did not suppose," testifies the witness, "that so solid a firm, as I considered the Griffels to be, would take in our institution. We had full confidence in them. When the Griffels went bankrupt it came to my knowledge that some of the savings bank's notes were in circulation. I looked into it and established that this was in fact so. I therefore went at once to Kraków where the Griffels were staying and demanded explanations categorically. I was at Dr Arnold's and there I threatened the Griffels that if they did not settle the matter I would shoot them." Dr Woźniakowski assured him the matter would be settled. Negotiations followed, as a result of which the Griffels declared they would give the bank security: IDA, WIFE OF THE LATE EISIG CHAIM GRIFFEL, was the owner of a tenement house in ul. Pieracki in Stanisławów, on part of which the bank registered a charge and so obtained 4,000 dollars. During his stay in Kraków the witness learned that there existed promissory notes accepted by the firm Silbergleit of Gliwice for about 600,000 dollars; that firm for a commission placed its signatures on the Griffels' notes, which the Griffels then put into circulation. PRESIDING JUDGE: "Mr Weissblum! State whether you were at the Savings Bank to collect the notes from the director?" The accused Weissblum is indignant and denies it categorically, maintaining he collected no notes from the director. Director Ziobrowski notes for his part that apart from Weissblum he had dealings with nobody from the Griffels' staff. While the defender Dr Landau was putting questions to the director, THE ACCUSED OZJASZ GRIFFEL SUDDENLY ROSE FROM THE BENCH, PALE, AND ASKED THE PRESIDING JUDGE'S LEAVE TO LEAVE THE ROOM; having received it he went out unsteadily, and when in the corridor he fell and fainted. The presiding judge broke off the hearing; the fainting Griffel was carried into a room and Dr Drzewicki was called, who on examination diagnosed weakness and nervous strain. The presiding judge adjourned until 5 in the afternoon. In the afternoon the first witness was Leib Wieselman, who dealt in oil terrains, buying them for the Griffels. He conducted a series of businesses in partnership with the late Griffel and was the chief supplier of oil terrains to the firm; he travelled constantly, sold and bought terrains, and enjoyed great esteem with old Griffel. Next testified DR MICHAŁ LAMM, former industrialist, owner of the Lamm Banking House in Stanisławów. He had known the late Eisig Chaim Griffel for several decades. When in 1928 the banks refused the Griffels credit, because he would not join the cartel and his consumption and freight credits were struck out, Griffel turned to the Lamms for help, which they gave. The struggle with the cartel ended in the Griffels' victory. The Lamms gave him bank currency, the sums sometimes reaching 80,000 dollars. After some months the Griffels signed an agreement with the cartel and for a time did not use the Lamms' credit. In 1929 Griffel began to work with Romania and again turned to the Lamms, who continued to finance that business. At the beginning of 1929 old Griffel fell ill and Dr Lamm lost contact with him; from then on the business was handled by the accused son Szulim Griffel. "On the whole transaction with the Griffels," testifies the witness, "we lost 40,000 dollars, and on account of our debt we have to this day not received a penny." Former clerks of the Griffels — Hersz Issakower, Hersz Kawaler and Michał Schwalb — then testified; their evidence adds nothing essential. After the witnesses were exhausted there was suddenly a short circuit in the court building, causing a break. After it the presiding judge began reading the documents. The defence made a series of applications; considering them will probably require adjournment until Friday.
*criminal trial · Ozjasz faints in court*

**13 July 1935 — Chwila, year 17, no. 5858 (13 July 1935)**  
THE GRIFFEL TRIAL HAS BEEN ADJOURNED. Stanisławów, 13 July. As we already reported in the evening Chwila, after a series of applications by prosecutor Goertz and the defenders Dr Landau and Guertler, the tribunal retired to deliberate for several hours. As a result the tribunal decided to fine the witness Joel Ehrlich 200 zł, convertible to 15 days' arrest, for failing to appear; after which the clerk read the tribunal's decisions on the applications. The tribunal agreed to the additional examination of 20 witnesses, among them director Adlersberg in Vienna, to be examined by letters rogatory, the director of the Discount Bank in Kraków Leon Pinz, the retired treasury councillor Dr Bodek, the oilman Witeles, and Majer Haller for a second time, and others. The tribunal further decided TO SEAL THE PREMISES HOUSING THE COMMERCIAL BOOKS OF THE FIRM GRIFFEL, and to take possession of the whole arsenal of Griffel promissory notes in the possession of N. Mesinger, and to adjourn the trial indefinitely.
*criminal trial · adjourned indefinitely*

**15 July 1935 — Kurier Lwowski, no. 193, 15 July 1935, year 7**  
STANISŁAWÓW CHRONICLE. ECHOES OF THE TIMBER WORKERS' STRIKE. At Demnia Wyżna a strike broke out among the timber workers of THE FIRM BRACIA GRIFFEL (Griffel Brothers), who, wishing to force the management to meet their demands, decided to shut down the power station supplying the firm with lighting and industrial current. To that end about 40 workers occupied the power-station building. Thanks, however, to the intervention of the firm's representatives, the strikers agreed, in view of the power station's higher public utility, to its operation, with the reservation that it would work only at night. — MOB JUSTICE BY JEWS AGAINST A CO-RELIGIONIST SWINDLER.
